Yes… I have. I still go, you know, to book club, and there’s a dance night I’ve gone to for years—though half the time we mostly gossip by the refreshment table more than dance, if I’m honest. And I do make myself show up because I think, well, if I stop going then everyone will worry.

Even my garden… usually I get such pleasure out of fussing over the tomatoes or cutting herbs, and cooking something for the neighbors used to give me this little lift. Now I do it, but it just doesn’t feel the same.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I haven’t been trying to lose weight, but food just doesn’t really call to me lately. I’ll make a piece of toast, or maybe a little soup, and then somehow it’s evening and I realize that’s almost all I’ve had.\n\nIt’s funny, because I used to be the one who’d insist everyone eat something—my sister used to tease me about always pushing leftovers on people. But for myself now, I just don’t seem to think about it much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless, exactly. That sounds so… final, doesn’t it? I do have moments where I look ahead and it feels awfully quiet, but then I’ll remember I need to water the geraniums or I promised someone I’d bring back their casserole dish.

And I do make myself show up because I think, well, if I stop going then everyone will worry.\n\nBut lately I’m there and it feels like I’m sort of behind a pane of glass, watching everyone else enjoy themselves. Even my garden… usually I get such pleasure out of fussing over the tomatoes or cutting herbs, and cooking something for the neighbors used to give me this little lift. Now I do it, but it just doesn’t feel the same.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I haven’t been trying to lose weight, but food just doesn’t really call to me lately. I’ll make a piece of toast, or maybe a little soup, and then somehow it’s evening and I realize that’s almost all I’ve had.\n\nIt’s funny, because I used to be the one who’d insist everyone eat something—my sister used to tease me about always pushing leftovers on people. But for myself now, I just don’t seem to think about it much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling slowed down or restless since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I do sit longer than I used to, but then again I’ve always been one to sit with a cup of tea and look out the window—my sister used to say I could make an afternoon disappear that way. Restless… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed pacing or anything like that. Maybe I’m just moving through the day differently, but I couldn’t say clearly one way or the other.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt hopeless about the future since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless, exactly. That sounds so… final, doesn’t it? I do have moments where I look ahead and it feels awfully quiet, but then I’ll remember I need to water the geraniums or I promised someone I’d bring back their casserole dish.\n\nMy sister and I used to talk about silly future things—like whether we’d become the sort of old ladies who wore purple hats to lunch. I suppose when I think about that, it’s hard not to feel a bit… blank. But hopeless? I’m not sure. I don’t think I can answer that neatly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I keep thinking about those last weeks with her, turning it over and over, like when you misplace a recipe card and keep checking the same drawer even though you know it isn’t there.\n\nI tell myself I should have called more often, or sat and really listened better when she was talking. Maybe I should have done something more useful instead of fussing with little things—tea, blankets, all that. Part of me knows I probably couldn’t have changed what happened, but that doesn’t seem to stop me from going back over it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I… I don’t know the exact number. Not every day, I don’t think. But enough mornings that it frightened me into asking for help. I wake up and there’s this thought that maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up at all.

I mean, I do sit longer than I used to, but then again I’ve always been one to sit with a cup of tea and look out the window—my sister used to say I could make an afternoon disappear that way. Restless… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed pacing or anything like that. Maybe I’m just moving through the day differently, but I couldn’t say clearly one way or the other.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt hopeless about the future since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless, exactly. That sounds so… final, doesn’t it? I do have moments where I look ahead and it feels awfully quiet, but then I’ll remember I need to water the geraniums or I promised someone I’d bring back their casserole dish.\n\nMy sister and I used to talk about silly future things—like whether we’d become the sort of old ladies who wore purple hats to lunch. I suppose when I think about that, it’s hard not to feel a bit… blank. But hopeless? I’m not sure. I don’t think I can answer that neatly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I keep thinking about those last weeks with her, turning it over and over, like when you misplace a recipe card and keep checking the same drawer even though you know it isn’t there.\n\nI tell myself I should have called more often, or sat and really listened better when she was talking. Maybe I should have done something more useful instead of fussing with little things—tea, blankets, all that. Part of me knows I probably couldn’t have changed what happened, but that doesn’t seem to stop me from going back over it.\n\nDoctor: How many days in the past month have you felt this way, with thoughts of not wanting to wake up and feeling behind a pane of glass?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know the exact number. Not every day, I don’t think. But enough mornings that it frightened me into asking for help. I wake up and there’s this thought that maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up at all.\n\nI should say—I don’t have a plan to hurt myself. It’s not like that, exactly. It’s more like being behind a pane of glass, watching the day start without me. Some mornings it passes once I get up and put the kettle on, or notice the mail needs bringing in… but it has happened several times this past month.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF33.1, F33.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose what finally got me to call is that some mornings I wake up—or I’m lying there before I’m fully up—and I think, it might just be easier if I hadn’t woken up at all. And then I scare myself a bit, because I’m not the kind of person who usually talks that way.\n\nI don’t have some plan to hurt myself or anything like that. It’s more... that thought keeps showing up, and I thought maybe I shouldn’t just keep brushing it off.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not wanting to wake up?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It started after my sister died—she died unexpectedly, and since then mornings have just been... different. I keep thinking of calling her, you know, because we had this silly habit of checking in about the weather, of all things. And then I remember. So yes, I’d say it’s been going on since then, about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in the things you normally enjoy doing since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I still go, you know, to book club, and there’s a dance night I’ve gone to for years—though half the time we mostly gossip by the refreshment table more than dance, if I’m honest. And I do make myself show up because I think, well, if I stop going then everyone will worry.\n\nBut lately I’m there and it feels like I’m sort of behind a pane of glass, watching everyone else enjoy themselves. Even my garden… usually I get such pleasure out of fussing over the tomatoes or cutting herbs, and cooking something for the neighbors used to give me this little lift. Now I do it, but it just doesn’t feel the same.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I haven’t been trying to lose weight, but food just doesn’t really call to me lately. I’ll make a piece of toast, or maybe a little soup, and then somehow it’s evening and I realize that’s almost all I’ve had.\n\nIt’s funny, because I used to be the one who’d insist everyone eat something—my sister used to tease me about always pushing leftovers on people. But for myself now, I just don’t seem to think about it much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling slowed down or restless since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I do sit longer than I used to, but then again I’ve always been one to sit with a cup of tea and look out the window—my sister used to say I could make an afternoon disappear that way. Restless… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed pacing or anything like that. Maybe I’m just moving through the day differently, but I couldn’t say clearly one way or the other.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt hopeless about the future since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless, exactly. That sounds so… final, doesn’t it? I do have moments where I look ahead and it feels awfully quiet, but then I’ll remember I need to water the geraniums or I promised someone I’d bring back their casserole dish.\n\nMy sister and I used to talk about silly future things—like whether we’d become the sort of old ladies who wore purple hats to lunch. I suppose when I think about that, it’s hard not to feel a bit… blank. But hopeless? I’m not sure. I don’t think I can answer that neatly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I keep thinking about those last weeks with her, turning it over and over, like when you misplace a recipe card and keep checking the same drawer even though you know it isn’t there.\n\nI tell myself I should have called more often, or sat and really listened better when she was talking. Maybe I should have done something more useful instead of fussing with little things—tea, blankets, all that. Part of me knows I probably couldn’t have changed what happened, but that doesn’t seem to stop me from going back over it.\n\nDoctor: How many days in the past month have you felt this way, with thoughts of not wanting to wake up and feeling behind a pane of glass?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know the exact number. Not every day, I don’t think. But enough mornings that it frightened me into asking for help. I wake up and there’s this thought that maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up at all.\n\nI should say—I don’t have a plan to hurt myself. It’s not like that, exactly. It’s more like being behind a pane of glass, watching the day start without me. Some mornings it passes once I get up and put the kettle on, or notice the mail needs bringing in… but it has happened several times this past month.\n\nDoctor: How many weeks have you been feeling this way, with thoughts of not wanting to wake up and feeling disconnected from activities?\n\nPatient: About four weeks, I think… since my sister died. It was so sudden, and I keep measuring time by all these little things—like the sympathy cards still sitting on the side table, and someone brought a lemon cake I never did finish.\n\nBut yes, to answer you properly, it’s been about a month that I’ve had those thoughts and felt so separate from things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you normally find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I still make myself go to things, like book club, and there’s that dance night—though I mostly just sit and watch now. Last time someone was talking about a novel with a missing letter in it, and I kept thinking about how my sister would’ve said the ending was too tidy.\n\nBut, sorry… yes, the answer is yes. I’m there, but it feels like I’m behind that pane of glass again. Even my garden doesn’t give me that little lift it used to, and cooking something for the neighbors just feels like going through the motions.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

So yes, I’d say it’s been going on since then, about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in the things you normally enjoy doing since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I still go, you know, to book club, and there’s a dance night I’ve gone to for years—though half the time we mostly gossip by the refreshment table more than dance, if I’m honest. Time’s gone strange, honestly—there are still little things of hers I keep thinking I should tell her about, and then I remember.\n\nBut yes, at least two weeks. It’s been going on most days for about a month.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you felt worthless since your sister passed away?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 69 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Worthlessness_Guilt\nmanifestation: \"I keep going over the last weeks with my sister and telling myself I should have called more, listened better, done something more useful, even when part of me knows I couldn't have changed what happened.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I have. Not all the time in some grand way, but I keep turning over those last weeks with her, like if I look at them enough I’ll find the place where I failed.

I tell myself I should’ve called more, or listened better when she was tired, or done something actually useful. And then I’ll remember something silly, like she’d asked me about a recipe and I never wrote it down for her—though that probably has nothing to do with anything.

Part of me knows I couldn’t have changed what happened. But it still feels like… I should have done more.

It started after my sister died—she died unexpectedly, and since then mornings have just been... different. So yes, I’d say it’s been going on since then, about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in the things you normally enjoy doing since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I still go, you know, to book club, and there’s a dance night I’ve gone to for years—though half the time we mostly gossip by the refreshment table more than dance, if I’m honest. And I do make myself show up because I think, well, if I stop going then everyone will worry.\n\nBut lately I’m there and it feels like I’m sort of behind a pane of glass, watching everyone else enjoy themselves. Even my garden… usually I get such pleasure out of fussing over the tomatoes or cutting herbs, and cooking something for the neighbors used to give me this little lift. Now I do it, but it just doesn’t feel the same.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I haven’t been trying to lose weight, but food just doesn’t really call to me lately. I’ll make a piece of toast, or maybe a little soup, and then somehow it’s evening and I realize that’s almost all I’ve had.\n\nIt’s funny, because I used to be the one who’d insist everyone eat something—my sister used to tease me about always pushing leftovers on people. But for myself now, I just don’t seem to think about it much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling slowed down or restless since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I do sit longer than I used to, but then again I’ve always been one to sit with a cup of tea and look out the window—my sister used to say I could make an afternoon disappear that way. Restless… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed pacing or anything like that. Maybe I’m just moving through the day differently, but I couldn’t say clearly one way or the other.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt hopeless about the future since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless, exactly. That sounds so… final, doesn’t it? I suppose when I think about that, it’s hard not to feel a bit… blank. But hopeless? I’m not sure. I don’t think I can answer that neatly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… not like I’m making plans, no. I should say that clearly. I don’t have a plan to hurt myself.

But I have had mornings where I wake up and think… it might’ve been easier if I just hadn’t. If I’d just slept through, you know? And then I feel awful even saying that, because my sister—well, she would’ve hated hearing me talk that way. She used to call me in the mornings sometimes, always too cheerful before coffee.

It’s more... that thought keeps showing up, and I thought maybe I shouldn’t just keep brushing it off.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not wanting to wake up?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It started after my sister died—she died unexpectedly, and since then mornings have just been... different. I keep thinking of calling her, you know, because we had this silly habit of checking in about the weather, of all things. And then I remember. So yes, I’d say it’s been going on since then, about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in the things you normally enjoy doing since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I still go, you know, to book club, and there’s a dance night I’ve gone to for years—though half the time we mostly gossip by the refreshment table more than dance, if I’m honest. And I do make myself show up because I think, well, if I stop going then everyone will worry.\n\nBut lately I’m there and it feels like I’m sort of behind a pane of glass, watching everyone else enjoy themselves. Even my garden… usually I get such pleasure out of fussing over the tomatoes or cutting herbs, and cooking something for the neighbors used to give me this little lift. I haven’t been trying to lose weight, but food just doesn’t really call to me lately. I’ll make a piece of toast, or maybe a little soup, and then somehow it’s evening and I realize that’s almost all I’ve had.\n\nIt’s funny, because I used to be the one who’d insist everyone eat something—my sister used to tease me about always pushing leftovers on people. But for myself now, I just don’t seem to think about it much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling slowed down or restless since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I do sit longer than I used to, but then again I’ve always been one to sit with a cup of tea and look out the window—my sister used to say I could make an afternoon disappear that way. Restless… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed pacing or anything like that. Maybe I’m just moving through the day differently, but I couldn’t say clearly one way or the other.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt hopeless about the future since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless, exactly. That sounds so… final, doesn’t it? I do have moments where I look ahead and it feels awfully quiet, but then I’ll remember I need to water the geraniums or I promised someone I’d bring back their casserole dish.\n\nMy sister and I used to talk about silly future things—like whether we’d become the sort of old ladies who wore purple hats to lunch. I suppose when I think about that, it’s hard not to feel a bit… blank. But hopeless? I’m not sure. I don’t think I can answer that neatly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I keep thinking about those last weeks with her, turning it over and over, like when you misplace a recipe card and keep checking the same drawer even though you know it isn’t there.\n\nI tell myself I should have called more often, or sat and really listened better when she was talking. Maybe I should have done something more useful instead of fussing with little things—tea, blankets, all that. Part of me knows I probably couldn’t have changed what happened, but that doesn’t seem to stop me from going back over it.\n\nDoctor: How many days in the past month have you felt this way, with thoughts of not wanting to wake up and feeling behind a pane of glass?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know the exact number. Not every day, I don’t think. But enough mornings that it frightened me into asking for help. It’s more like being behind a pane of glass, watching the day start without me. I still make myself go to things, like book club, and there’s that dance night—though I mostly just sit and watch now. I’m there, but it feels like I’m behind that pane of glass again. Even my garden doesn’t give me that little lift it used to, and cooking something for the neighbors just feels like going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Have you had at least two weeks where you felt this way nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I have. It’s strange, because I’ll think, “Oh, I’ll just run one errand,” or “I’ll just get those papers together for the Cultural Center,” and then it feels like I’ve done some enormous thing.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose what finally got me to call is that some mornings I wake up—or I’m lying there before I’m fully up—and I think, it might just be easier if I hadn’t woken up at all. And then I scare myself a bit, because I’m not the kind of person who usually talks that way.\n\nI don’t have some plan to hurt myself or anything like that. It’s more... that thought keeps showing up, and I thought maybe I shouldn’t just keep brushing it off.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not wanting to wake up?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It started after my sister died—she died unexpectedly, and since then mornings have just been... different. I keep thinking of calling her, you know, because we had this silly habit of checking in about the weather, of all things. And then I remember. So yes, I’d say it’s been going on since then, about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in the things you normally enjoy doing since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I still go, you know, to book club, and there’s a dance night I’ve gone to for years—though half the time we mostly gossip by the refreshment table more than dance, if I’m honest. And I do make myself show up because I think, well, if I stop going then everyone will worry.\n\nBut lately I’m there and it feels like I’m sort of behind a pane of glass, watching everyone else enjoy themselves. Even my garden… usually I get such pleasure out of fussing over the tomatoes or cutting herbs, and cooking something for the neighbors used to give me this little lift. Now I do it, but it just doesn’t feel the same.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I haven’t been trying to lose weight, but food just doesn’t really call to me lately. I’ll make a piece of toast, or maybe a little soup, and then somehow it’s evening and I realize that’s almost all I’ve had.\n\nIt’s funny, because I used to be the one who’d insist everyone eat something—my sister used to tease me about always pushing leftovers on people. But for myself now, I just don’t seem to think about it much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling slowed down or restless since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I do sit longer than I used to, but then again I’ve always been one to sit with a cup of tea and look out the window—my sister used to say I could make an afternoon disappear that way. Restless… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed pacing or anything like that. Maybe I’m just moving through the day differently, but I couldn’t say clearly one way or the other.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt hopeless about the future since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless, exactly. That sounds so… final, doesn’t it? I do have moments where I look ahead and it feels awfully quiet, but then I’ll remember I need to water the geraniums or I promised someone I’d bring back their casserole dish.\n\nMy sister and I used to talk about silly future things—like whether we’d become the sort of old ladies who wore purple hats to lunch. I suppose when I think about that, it’s hard not to feel a bit… blank. But hopeless? I’m not sure. I don’t think I can answer that neatly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I keep thinking about those last weeks with her, turning it over and over, like when you misplace a recipe card and keep checking the same drawer even though you know it isn’t there.\n\nI tell myself I should have called more often, or sat and really listened better when she was talking. Maybe I should have done something more useful instead of fussing with little things—tea, blankets, all that. Part of me knows I probably couldn’t have changed what happened, but that doesn’t seem to stop me from going back over it.\n\nDoctor: How many days in the past month have you felt this way, with thoughts of not wanting to wake up and feeling behind a pane of glass?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know the exact number. Not every day, I don’t think. But enough mornings that it frightened me into asking for help. I wake up and there’s this thought that maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up at all.\n\nI should say—I don’t have a plan to hurt myself. It’s not like that, exactly. It’s more like being behind a pane of glass, watching the day start without me. Some mornings it passes once I get up and put the kettle on, or notice the mail needs bringing in… but it has happened several times this past month.\n\nDoctor: How many weeks have you been feeling this way, with thoughts of not wanting to wake up and feeling disconnected from activities?\n\nPatient: About four weeks, I think… since my sister died. It was so sudden, and I keep measuring time by all these little things—like the sympathy cards still sitting on the side table, and someone brought a lemon cake I never did finish.\n\nBut yes, to answer you properly, it’s been about a month that I’ve had those thoughts and felt so separate from things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you normally find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I still make myself go to things, like book club, and there’s that dance night—though I mostly just sit and watch now. Last time someone was talking about a novel with a missing letter in it, and I kept thinking about how my sister would’ve said the ending was too tidy.\n\nBut, sorry… yes, the answer is yes. I’m there, but it feels like I’m behind that pane of glass again. Even my garden doesn’t give me that little lift it used to, and cooking something for the neighbors just feels like going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Have you had at least two weeks where you felt this way nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. It’s been about a month now, since my sister died so suddenly. Time’s gone strange, honestly—there are still little things of hers I keep thinking I should tell her about, and then I remember.\n\nBut yes, at least two weeks. It’s been going on most days for about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt worthless since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Not all the time in some grand way, but I keep turning over those last weeks with her, like if I look at them enough I’ll find the place where I failed.\n\nI tell myself I should’ve called more, or listened better when she was tired, or done something actually useful. And then I’ll remember something silly, like she’d asked me about a recipe and I never wrote it down for her—though that probably has nothing to do with anything.\n\nPart of me knows I couldn’t have changed what happened. But it still feels like… I should have done more.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts of death or dying frequently since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… not like I’m making plans, no. I should say that clearly. I don’t have a plan to hurt myself.\n\nBut I have had mornings where I wake up and think… it might’ve been easier if I just hadn’t. If I’d just slept through, you know? And then I feel awful even saying that, because my sister—well, she would’ve hated hearing me talk that way. She used to call me in the mornings sometimes, always too cheerful before coffee.\n\nBut yes. That’s what frightened me enough to come here, really. Those thoughts have been coming up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. Even small tasks wear me out more than they should.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you lost a significant amount of weight since your sister passed away?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 69 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Weight_Appetite_Change\nmanifestation: \"Food just doesn't call to me much anymore, so I'll fix a little toast or soup and then realize by evening I've barely eaten, even though I'm not trying to lose weight.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if it’s a “significant” amount… I haven’t been carefully tracking it. But food just doesn’t really call to me much anymore.

I’ll make a piece of toast, or some soup, and think that’s enough for now—and then by evening I realize I’ve barely eaten. I’m not trying to lose weight. It’s just… eating feels like something I forget how to care about.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose what finally got me to call is that some mornings I wake up—or I’m lying there before I’m fully up—and I think, it might just be easier if I hadn’t woken up at all. And then I scare myself a bit, because I’m not the kind of person who usually talks that way.\n\nI don’t have some plan to hurt myself or anything like that. It’s more... that thought keeps showing up, and I thought maybe I shouldn’t just keep brushing it off.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not wanting to wake up?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It started after my sister died—she died unexpectedly, and since then mornings have just been... different. I keep thinking of calling her, you know, because we had this silly habit of checking in about the weather, of all things. And then I remember. So yes, I’d say it’s been going on since then, about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in the things you normally enjoy doing since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I still go, you know, to book club, and there’s a dance night I’ve gone to for years—though half the time we mostly gossip by the refreshment table more than dance, if I’m honest. And I do make myself show up because I think, well, if I stop going then everyone will worry.\n\nBut lately I’m there and it feels like I’m sort of behind a pane of glass, watching everyone else enjoy themselves. Even my garden… usually I get such pleasure out of fussing over the tomatoes or cutting herbs, and cooking something for the neighbors used to give me this little lift. Now I do it, but it just doesn’t feel the same.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I haven’t been trying to lose weight, but food just doesn’t really call to me lately. I’ll make a piece of toast, or maybe a little soup, and then somehow it’s evening and I realize that’s almost all I’ve had.\n\nIt’s funny, because I used to be the one who’d insist everyone eat something—my sister used to tease me about always pushing leftovers on people. But for myself now, I just don’t seem to think about it much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling slowed down or restless since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I do sit longer than I used to, but then again I’ve always been one to sit with a cup of tea and look out the window—my sister used to say I could make an afternoon disappear that way. Restless… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed pacing or anything like that. Maybe I’m just moving through the day differently, but I couldn’t say clearly one way or the other.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt hopeless about the future since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless, exactly. That sounds so… final, doesn’t it? I do have moments where I look ahead and it feels awfully quiet, but then I’ll remember I need to water the geraniums or I promised someone I’d bring back their casserole dish.\n\nMy sister and I used to talk about silly future things—like whether we’d become the sort of old ladies who wore purple hats to lunch. I suppose when I think about that, it’s hard not to feel a bit… blank. But hopeless? I’m not sure. I don’t think I can answer that neatly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I keep thinking about those last weeks with her, turning it over and over, like when you misplace a recipe card and keep checking the same drawer even though you know it isn’t there.\n\nI tell myself I should have called more often, or sat and really listened better when she was talking. Maybe I should have done something more useful instead of fussing with little things—tea, blankets, all that. Part of me knows I probably couldn’t have changed what happened, but that doesn’t seem to stop me from going back over it.\n\nDoctor: How many days in the past month have you felt this way, with thoughts of not wanting to wake up and feeling behind a pane of glass?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know the exact number. Not every day, I don’t think. But enough mornings that it frightened me into asking for help. I wake up and there’s this thought that maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up at all.\n\nI should say—I don’t have a plan to hurt myself. It’s not like that, exactly. It’s more like being behind a pane of glass, watching the day start without me. Some mornings it passes once I get up and put the kettle on, or notice the mail needs bringing in… but it has happened several times this past month.\n\nDoctor: How many weeks have you been feeling this way, with thoughts of not wanting to wake up and feeling disconnected from activities?\n\nPatient: About four weeks, I think… since my sister died. It was so sudden, and I keep measuring time by all these little things—like the sympathy cards still sitting on the side table, and someone brought a lemon cake I never did finish.\n\nBut yes, to answer you properly, it’s been about a month that I’ve had those thoughts and felt so separate from things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you normally find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I still make myself go to things, like book club, and there’s that dance night—though I mostly just sit and watch now. Last time someone was talking about a novel with a missing letter in it, and I kept thinking about how my sister would’ve said the ending was too tidy.\n\nBut, sorry… yes, the answer is yes. I’m there, but it feels like I’m behind that pane of glass again. Even my garden doesn’t give me that little lift it used to, and cooking something for the neighbors just feels like going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Have you had at least two weeks where you felt this way nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. It’s been about a month now, since my sister died so suddenly. Time’s gone strange, honestly—there are still little things of hers I keep thinking I should tell her about, and then I remember.\n\nBut yes, at least two weeks. It’s been going on most days for about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt worthless since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Not all the time in some grand way, but I keep turning over those last weeks with her, like if I look at them enough I’ll find the place where I failed.\n\nI tell myself I should’ve called more, or listened better when she was tired, or done something actually useful. And then I’ll remember something silly, like she’d asked me about a recipe and I never wrote it down for her—though that probably has nothing to do with anything.\n\nPart of me knows I couldn’t have changed what happened. But it still feels like… I should have done more.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts of death or dying frequently since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… not like I’m making plans, no. I should say that clearly. I don’t have a plan to hurt myself.\n\nBut I have had mornings where I wake up and think… it might’ve been easier if I just hadn’t. If I’d just slept through, you know? And then I feel awful even saying that, because my sister—well, she would’ve hated hearing me talk that way. She used to call me in the mornings sometimes, always too cheerful before coffee.\n\nBut yes. That’s what frightened me enough to come here, really. Those thoughts have been coming up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. It’s strange, because I’ll think, “Oh, I’ll just run one errand,” or “I’ll just get those papers together for the Cultural Center,” and then it feels like I’ve done some enormous thing.\n\nMy body feels slow, like I’m moving through thick mud. Even small tasks wear me out more than they should.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s a “significant” amount… I haven’t been carefully tracking it. Those thoughts have been coming up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. It’s strange, because I’ll think, “Oh, I’ll just run one errand,” or “I’ll just get those papers together for the Cultural Center,” and then it feels like I’ve done some enormous thing.\n\nMy body feels slow, like I’m moving through thick mud. Even small tasks wear me out more than they should.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s a “significant” amount… I haven’t been carefully tracking it. But food just doesn’t really call to me much anymore.\n\nI’ll make a piece of toast, or some soup, and think that’s enough for now—and then by evening I realize I’ve barely eaten. I’m not trying to lose weight. It’s just… eating feels like something I forget how to care about.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you felt that these thoughts of not wanting to wake up are interfering with your daily life most days?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 69 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I have had to ask another volunteer to cover parts of the Juneteenth budgeting workshop because I cannot keep up with the preparation the way I normally would, and I have been turning down neighbors and friends more than usual.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think they are. Not every single moment, but most days it sort of throws me off before I’ve even started.

I had to ask another volunteer to take over parts of the Juneteenth budgeting workshop, which… I hate doing that. I’m usually the one with the folders and the little color-coded notes—my sister used to tease me about that, actually, said I could organize a tornado. But I just can’t keep up with the preparation the way I normally would.

And I’ve been turning down neighbors and friends more than usual. Little invitations, stopping by, helping with things. I just… I don’t have the room in me for it lately.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF33.1, F33.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose what finally got me to call is that some mornings I wake up—or I’m lying there before I’m fully up—and I think, it might just be easier if I hadn’t woken up at all. And then I scare myself a bit, because I’m not the kind of person who usually talks that way.\n\nI don’t have some plan to hurt myself or anything like that. It’s more... that thought keeps showing up, and I thought maybe I shouldn’t just keep brushing it off.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not wanting to wake up?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It started after my sister died—she died unexpectedly, and since then mornings have just been... different. I keep thinking of calling her, you know, because we had this silly habit of checking in about the weather, of all things. And then I remember. So yes, I’d say it’s been going on since then, about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in the things you normally enjoy doing since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I still go, you know, to book club, and there’s a dance night I’ve gone to for years—though half the time we mostly gossip by the refreshment table more than dance, if I’m honest. And I do make myself show up because I think, well, if I stop going then everyone will worry.\n\nBut lately I’m there and it feels like I’m sort of behind a pane of glass, watching everyone else enjoy themselves. Even my garden… usually I get such pleasure out of fussing over the tomatoes or cutting herbs, and cooking something for the neighbors used to give me this little lift. Now I do it, but it just doesn’t feel the same.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I haven’t been trying to lose weight, but food just doesn’t really call to me lately. I’ll make a piece of toast, or maybe a little soup, and then somehow it’s evening and I realize that’s almost all I’ve had.\n\nIt’s funny, because I used to be the one who’d insist everyone eat something—my sister used to tease me about always pushing leftovers on people. But for myself now, I just don’t seem to think about it much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling slowed down or restless since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I do sit longer than I used to, but then again I’ve always been one to sit with a cup of tea and look out the window—my sister used to say I could make an afternoon disappear that way. Restless… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed pacing or anything like that. Maybe I’m just moving through the day differently, but I couldn’t say clearly one way or the other.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt hopeless about the future since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless, exactly. That sounds so… final, doesn’t it? I do have moments where I look ahead and it feels awfully quiet, but then I’ll remember I need to water the geraniums or I promised someone I’d bring back their casserole dish.\n\nMy sister and I used to talk about silly future things—like whether we’d become the sort of old ladies who wore purple hats to lunch. I suppose when I think about that, it’s hard not to feel a bit… blank. But hopeless? I’m not sure. I don’t think I can answer that neatly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I keep thinking about those last weeks with her, turning it over and over, like when you misplace a recipe card and keep checking the same drawer even though you know it isn’t there.\n\nI tell myself I should have called more often, or sat and really listened better when she was talking. Maybe I should have done something more useful instead of fussing with little things—tea, blankets, all that. Part of me knows I probably couldn’t have changed what happened, but that doesn’t seem to stop me from going back over it.\n\nDoctor: How many days in the past month have you felt this way, with thoughts of not wanting to wake up and feeling behind a pane of glass?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know the exact number. Not every day, I don’t think. But enough mornings that it frightened me into asking for help. I wake up and there’s this thought that maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up at all.\n\nI should say—I don’t have a plan to hurt myself. It’s not like that, exactly. It’s more like being behind a pane of glass, watching the day start without me. Some mornings it passes once I get up and put the kettle on, or notice the mail needs bringing in… but it has happened several times this past month.\n\nDoctor: How many weeks have you been feeling this way, with thoughts of not wanting to wake up and feeling disconnected from activities?\n\nPatient: About four weeks, I think… since my sister died. It was so sudden, and I keep measuring time by all these little things—like the sympathy cards still sitting on the side table, and someone brought a lemon cake I never did finish.\n\nBut yes, to answer you properly, it’s been about a month that I’ve had those thoughts and felt so separate from things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you normally find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I still make myself go to things, like book club, and there’s that dance night—though I mostly just sit and watch now. Last time someone was talking about a novel with a missing letter in it, and I kept thinking about how my sister would’ve said the ending was too tidy.\n\nBut, sorry… yes, the answer is yes. I’m there, but it feels like I’m behind that pane of glass again. Even my garden doesn’t give me that little lift it used to, and cooking something for the neighbors just feels like going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Have you had at least two weeks where you felt this way nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. It’s been about a month now, since my sister died so suddenly. Time’s gone strange, honestly—there are still little things of hers I keep thinking I should tell her about, and then I remember.\n\nBut yes, at least two weeks. It’s been going on most days for about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt worthless since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Not all the time in some grand way, but I keep turning over those last weeks with her, like if I look at them enough I’ll find the place where I failed.\n\nI tell myself I should’ve called more, or listened better when she was tired, or done something actually useful. And then I’ll remember something silly, like she’d asked me about a recipe and I never wrote it down for her—though that probably has nothing to do with anything.\n\nPart of me knows I couldn’t have changed what happened. But it still feels like… I should have done more.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts of death or dying frequently since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… not like I’m making plans, no. I should say that clearly. I don’t have a plan to hurt myself.\n\nBut I have had mornings where I wake up and think… it might’ve been easier if I just hadn’t. If I’d just slept through, you know? And then I feel awful even saying that, because my sister—well, she would’ve hated hearing me talk that way. She used to call me in the mornings sometimes, always too cheerful before coffee.\n\nBut yes. That’s what frightened me enough to come here, really. Those thoughts have been coming up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. It’s strange, because I’ll think, “Oh, I’ll just run one errand,” or “I’ll just get those papers together for the Cultural Center,” and then it feels like I’ve done some enormous thing.\n\nMy body feels slow, like I’m moving through thick mud. Even small tasks wear me out more than they should.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s a “significant” amount… I haven’t been carefully tracking it. But food just doesn’t really call to me much anymore.\n\nI’ll make a piece of toast, or some soup, and think that’s enough for now—and then by evening I realize I’ve barely eaten. I’m not trying to lose weight. It’s just… eating feels like something I forget how to care about.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt that these thoughts of not wanting to wake up are interfering with your daily life most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they are. Not every single moment, but most days it sort of throws me off before I’ve even started.\n\nI had to ask another volunteer to take over parts of the Juneteenth budgeting workshop, which… I hate doing that. There have been… a few quieter mornings, I suppose, where I can make coffee and look out the window and almost feel like myself for a little bit. But what scared me enough to come ask for help is that I keep having these mornings where I think, “Maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up.”

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well... And then I scare myself a bit, because I’m not the kind of person who usually talks that way.\n\nI don’t have some plan to hurt myself or anything like that. It’s more... that thought keeps showing up, and I thought maybe I shouldn’t just keep brushing it off.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not wanting to wake up?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It started after my sister died—she died unexpectedly, and since then mornings have just been... different. I keep thinking of calling her, you know, because we had this silly habit of checking in about the weather, of all things. And then I remember. So yes, I’d say it’s been going on since then, about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in the things you normally enjoy doing since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I still go, you know, to book club, and there’s a dance night I’ve gone to for years—though half the time we mostly gossip by the refreshment table more than dance, if I’m honest. And I do make myself show up because I think, well, if I stop going then everyone will worry.\n\nBut lately I’m there and it feels like I’m sort of behind a pane of glass, watching everyone else enjoy themselves. Even my garden… usually I get such pleasure out of fussing over the tomatoes or cutting herbs, and cooking something for the neighbors used to give me this little lift. Now I do it, but it just doesn’t feel the same.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I haven’t been trying to lose weight, but food just doesn’t really call to me lately. I’ll make a piece of toast, or maybe a little soup, and then somehow it’s evening and I realize that’s almost all I’ve had.\n\nIt’s funny, because I used to be the one who’d insist everyone eat something—my sister used to tease me about always pushing leftovers on people. But for myself now, I just don’t seem to think about it much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling slowed down or restless since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I do sit longer than I used to, but then again I’ve always been one to sit with a cup of tea and look out the window—my sister used to say I could make an afternoon disappear that way. Restless… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed pacing or anything like that. Maybe I’m just moving through the day differently, but I couldn’t say clearly one way or the other.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt hopeless about the future since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless, exactly. That sounds so… final, doesn’t it? I do have moments where I look ahead and it feels awfully quiet, but then I’ll remember I need to water the geraniums or I promised someone I’d bring back their casserole dish.\n\nMy sister and I used to talk about silly future things—like whether we’d become the sort of old ladies who wore purple hats to lunch. I suppose when I think about that, it’s hard not to feel a bit… blank. But hopeless? I’m not sure. I don’t think I can answer that neatly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I keep thinking about those last weeks with her, turning it over and over, like when you misplace a recipe card and keep checking the same drawer even though you know it isn’t there.\n\nI tell myself I should have called more often, or sat and really listened better when she was talking. Maybe I should have done something more useful instead of fussing with little things—tea, blankets, all that. Part of me knows I probably couldn’t have changed what happened, but that doesn’t seem to stop me from going back over it.\n\nDoctor: How many days in the past month have you felt this way, with thoughts of not wanting to wake up and feeling behind a pane of glass?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know the exact number. Not every day, I don’t think. But enough mornings that it frightened me into asking for help. I wake up and there’s this thought that maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up at all.\n\nI should say—I don’t have a plan to hurt myself. It’s not like that, exactly. It’s more like being behind a pane of glass, watching the day start without me. Some mornings it passes once I get up and put the kettle on, or notice the mail needs bringing in… but it has happened several times this past month.\n\nDoctor: How many weeks have you been feeling this way, with thoughts of not wanting to wake up and feeling disconnected from activities?\n\nPatient: About four weeks, I think… since my sister died. It was so sudden, and I keep measuring time by all these little things—like the sympathy cards still sitting on the side table, and someone brought a lemon cake I never did finish.\n\nBut yes, to answer you properly, it’s been about a month that I’ve had those thoughts and felt so separate from things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you normally find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I still make myself go to things, like book club, and there’s that dance night—though I mostly just sit and watch now. Last time someone was talking about a novel with a missing letter in it, and I kept thinking about how my sister would’ve said the ending was too tidy.\n\nBut, sorry… yes, the answer is yes. I’m there, but it feels like I’m behind that pane of glass again. Not all the time in some grand way, but I keep turning over those last weeks with her, like if I look at them enough I’ll find the place where I failed.\n\nI tell myself I should’ve called more, or listened better when she was tired, or done something actually useful. And then I’ll remember something silly, like she’d asked me about a recipe and I never wrote it down for her—though that probably has nothing to do with anything.\n\nPart of me knows I couldn’t have changed what happened. But it still feels like… I should have done more.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts of death or dying frequently since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… not like I’m making plans, no. No… not really for two whole weeks. This has really only been about a month altogether, since my sister died so suddenly.

There are little stretches where I think, oh, maybe I’m doing all right today—like if I’m folding laundry or watering the plant she gave me, which I’ve nearly killed twice—but it doesn’t last. I wouldn’t say there’s been a solid period where things lifted and stayed better.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF33.1, F33.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose what finally got me to call is that some mornings I wake up—or I’m lying there before I’m fully up—and I think, it might just be easier if I hadn’t woken up at all. And then I scare myself a bit, because I’m not the kind of person who usually talks that way.\n\nI don’t have some plan to hurt myself or anything like that. It’s more... that thought keeps showing up, and I thought maybe I shouldn’t just keep brushing it off.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not wanting to wake up?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It started after my sister died—she died unexpectedly, and since then mornings have just been... different. I keep thinking of calling her, you know, because we had this silly habit of checking in about the weather, of all things. And then I remember. So yes, I’d say it’s been going on since then, about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in the things you normally enjoy doing since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I still go, you know, to book club, and there’s a dance night I’ve gone to for years—though half the time we mostly gossip by the refreshment table more than dance, if I’m honest. And I do make myself show up because I think, well, if I stop going then everyone will worry.\n\nBut lately I’m there and it feels like I’m sort of behind a pane of glass, watching everyone else enjoy themselves. Even my garden… usually I get such pleasure out of fussing over the tomatoes or cutting herbs, and cooking something for the neighbors used to give me this little lift. Now I do it, but it just doesn’t feel the same.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I haven’t been trying to lose weight, but food just doesn’t really call to me lately. I’ll make a piece of toast, or maybe a little soup, and then somehow it’s evening and I realize that’s almost all I’ve had.\n\nIt’s funny, because I used to be the one who’d insist everyone eat something—my sister used to tease me about always pushing leftovers on people. But for myself now, I just don’t seem to think about it much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling slowed down or restless since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I do sit longer than I used to, but then again I’ve always been one to sit with a cup of tea and look out the window—my sister used to say I could make an afternoon disappear that way. Restless… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed pacing or anything like that. Maybe I’m just moving through the day differently, but I couldn’t say clearly one way or the other.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt hopeless about the future since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless, exactly. That sounds so… final, doesn’t it? I do have moments where I look ahead and it feels awfully quiet, but then I’ll remember I need to water the geraniums or I promised someone I’d bring back their casserole dish.\n\nMy sister and I used to talk about silly future things—like whether we’d become the sort of old ladies who wore purple hats to lunch. I suppose when I think about that, it’s hard not to feel a bit… blank. But hopeless? I’m not sure. I don’t think I can answer that neatly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I keep thinking about those last weeks with her, turning it over and over, like when you misplace a recipe card and keep checking the same drawer even though you know it isn’t there.\n\nI tell myself I should have called more often, or sat and really listened better when she was talking. Maybe I should have done something more useful instead of fussing with little things—tea, blankets, all that. Part of me knows I probably couldn’t have changed what happened, but that doesn’t seem to stop me from going back over it.\n\nDoctor: How many days in the past month have you felt this way, with thoughts of not wanting to wake up and feeling behind a pane of glass?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know the exact number. Not every day, I don’t think. But enough mornings that it frightened me into asking for help. I wake up and there’s this thought that maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up at all.\n\nI should say—I don’t have a plan to hurt myself. It’s not like that, exactly. It’s more like being behind a pane of glass, watching the day start without me. Some mornings it passes once I get up and put the kettle on, or notice the mail needs bringing in… but it has happened several times this past month.\n\nDoctor: How many weeks have you been feeling this way, with thoughts of not wanting to wake up and feeling disconnected from activities?\n\nPatient: About four weeks, I think… since my sister died. It was so sudden, and I keep measuring time by all these little things—like the sympathy cards still sitting on the side table, and someone brought a lemon cake I never did finish.\n\nBut yes, to answer you properly, it’s been about a month that I’ve had those thoughts and felt so separate from things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you normally find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I still make myself go to things, like book club, and there’s that dance night—though I mostly just sit and watch now. Last time someone was talking about a novel with a missing letter in it, and I kept thinking about how my sister would’ve said the ending was too tidy.\n\nBut, sorry… yes, the answer is yes. I’m there, but it feels like I’m behind that pane of glass again. Even my garden doesn’t give me that little lift it used to, and cooking something for the neighbors just feels like going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Have you had at least two weeks where you felt this way nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. It’s been about a month now, since my sister died so suddenly. Time’s gone strange, honestly—there are still little things of hers I keep thinking I should tell her about, and then I remember.\n\nBut yes, at least two weeks. It’s been going on most days for about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt worthless since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Not all the time in some grand way, but I keep turning over those last weeks with her, like if I look at them enough I’ll find the place where I failed.\n\nI tell myself I should’ve called more, or listened better when she was tired, or done something actually useful. And then I’ll remember something silly, like she’d asked me about a recipe and I never wrote it down for her—though that probably has nothing to do with anything.\n\nPart of me knows I couldn’t have changed what happened. But it still feels like… I should have done more.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts of death or dying frequently since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… not like I’m making plans, no. I should say that clearly. I don’t have a plan to hurt myself.\n\nBut I have had mornings where I wake up and think… it might’ve been easier if I just hadn’t. If I’d just slept through, you know? And then I feel awful even saying that, because my sister—well, she would’ve hated hearing me talk that way. She used to call me in the mornings sometimes, always too cheerful before coffee.\n\nBut yes. That’s what frightened me enough to come here, really. Those thoughts have been coming up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. It’s strange, because I’ll think, “Oh, I’ll just run one errand,” or “I’ll just get those papers together for the Cultural Center,” and then it feels like I’ve done some enormous thing.\n\nMy body feels slow, like I’m moving through thick mud. Even small tasks wear me out more than they should.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s a “significant” amount… I haven’t been carefully tracking it. But food just doesn’t really call to me much anymore.\n\nI’ll make a piece of toast, or some soup, and think that’s enough for now—and then by evening I realize I’ve barely eaten. I’m not trying to lose weight. It’s just… eating feels like something I forget how to care about.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt that these thoughts of not wanting to wake up are interfering with your daily life most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they are. Not every single moment, but most days it sort of throws me off before I’ve even started.\n\nI had to ask another volunteer to take over parts of the Juneteenth budgeting workshop, which… I hate doing that. I’m usually the one with the folders and the little color-coded notes—my sister used to tease me about that, actually, said I could organize a tornado. But I just can’t keep up with the preparation the way I normally would.\n\nAnd I’ve been turning down neighbors and friends more than usual. Little invitations, stopping by, helping with things. I just… I don’t have the room in me for it lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past month where you didn't have these thoughts of not wanting to wake up and felt somewhat normal?\n\nPatient: There have been… a few quieter mornings, I suppose, where I can make coffee and look out the window and almost feel like myself for a little bit. But what scared me enough to come ask for help is that I keep having these mornings where I think, “Maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up.”\n\nI don’t have a plan to hurt myself. I want to be clear about that. It’s more like the thought is just sitting there when I open my eyes. And then I think about my sister, and about how she always said the morning light in the kitchen was the best part of the house—funny what sticks with you—and I feel ashamed that I’m not appreciating it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of at least two weeks in the past month where you felt better and your symptoms improved?\n\nPatient: No… not really for two whole weeks. This has really only been about a month altogether, since my sister died so suddenly.\n\nThere are little stretches where I think, oh, maybe I’m doing all right today—like if I’m folding laundry or watering the plant she gave me, which I’ve nearly killed twice—but it doesn’t last. Not all the time in some grand way, but I keep turning over those last weeks with her, like if I look at them enough I’ll find the place where I failed.\n\nI tell myself I should’ve called more, or listened better when she was tired, or done something actually useful. And then I’ll remember something silly, like she’d asked me about a recipe and I never wrote it down for her—though that probably has nothing to do with anything.\n\nPart of me knows I couldn’t have changed what happened. But it still feels like… I should have done more.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts of death or dying frequently since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… not like I’m making plans, no. I should say that clearly. I don’t have a plan to hurt myself.\n\nBut I have had mornings where I wake up and think… it might’ve been easier if I just hadn’t. If I’d just slept through, you know? And then I feel awful even saying that, because my sister—well, she would’ve hated hearing me talk that way. She used to call me in the mornings sometimes, always too cheerful before coffee.\n\nBut yes. That’s what frightened me enough to come here, really. Those thoughts have been coming up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. It’s strange, because I’ll think, “Oh, I’ll just run one errand,” or “I’ll just get those papers together for the Cultural Center,” and then it feels like I’ve done some enormous thing.\n\nMy body feels slow, like I’m moving through thick mud. Even small tasks wear me out more than they should.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s a “significant” amount… I haven’t been carefully tracking it. But food just doesn’t really call to me much anymore.\n\nI’ll make a piece of toast, or some soup, and think that’s enough for now—and then by evening I realize I’ve barely eaten. I’m not trying to lose weight. It’s just… eating feels like something I forget how to care about.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt that these thoughts of not wanting to wake up are interfering with your daily life most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they are. Not every single moment, but most days it sort of throws me off before I’ve even started.\n\nI had to ask another volunteer to take over parts of the Juneteenth budgeting workshop, which… I hate doing that. I’m usually the one with the folders and the little color-coded notes—my sister used to tease me about that, actually, said I could organize a tornado. But I just can’t keep up with the preparation the way I normally would.\n\nAnd I’ve been turning down neighbors and friends more than usual. Little invitations, stopping by, helping with things. I just… I don’t have the room in me for it lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past month where you didn't have these thoughts of not wanting to wake up and felt somewhat normal?\n\nPatient: There have been… a few quieter mornings, I suppose, where I can make coffee and look out the window and almost feel like myself for a little bit. But what scared me enough to come ask for help is that I keep having these mornings where I think, “Maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up.”\n\nI don’t have a plan to hurt myself. I want to be clear about that. It’s more like the thought is just sitting there when I open my eyes. And then I think about my sister, and about how she always said the morning light in the kitchen was the best part of the house—funny what sticks with you—and I feel ashamed that I’m not appreciating it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of at least two weeks in the past month where you felt better and your symptoms improved?\n\nPatient: No… not really for two whole weeks. This has really only been about a month altogether, since my sister died so suddenly.\n\nThere are little stretches where I think, oh, maybe I’m doing all right today—like if I’m folding laundry or watering the plant she gave me, which I’ve nearly killed twice—but it doesn’t last.
